## S4 — Planning & multi-step execution

**Priority band:** P0 parity-core.

**Evidence base.** `agent-evidence.md` §4 Area 3 (F3-1 … F3-16) and §4 Area 3b; `agent-reference-shots/areas/area3-planning.md` (the per-finding detail); `docs-cache/permission-modes.md` (plan mode semantics); `docs-cache/common-workflows.md` (plan workflow); `area-interactive-mode/_section-interactive-mode.md` §2 F-I17 (the task-list widget and its five-item cap).

**Scope note.** S4 covers the *deliberative* tier: how a plan is produced, how it is approved, how work is decomposed into a task graph, and how the plan-mode invariant (no mutation) is enforced.
| ID | Evidence | Acceptance criterion (testable) | Depends on | Proposed module + interface shape | Automated verification test | Security / operational risk + mitigation | Priority / Phase |
| :- | :- | :- | :- | :- | :- | :- | :- |
| S4-PLAN-001 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-1; area3-planning.md F3-1; **claude-code-evidence.md §9.4.2**; `agent-reference-shots/area8-permission-modes/observed-behaviours.md` O6 | Plan mode is a permission mode, and while it is active the working tree is not mutated: no edit, write or mutating shell command is executed; a request to mutate is refused by the permission layer, not merely discouraged. | S6-MODE-020, **S4-PLAN-016** | `permissions/modes.py :: Mode` enum including `PLAN`; `permissions/engine.py :: decide(request, mode) -> Decision` where **mutating requests in `PLAN`** yield `Decision.deny(reason='plan_mode')`. "Mutating" is decided by the **operation class** (§ interface-spec 5.8), not by the tool name — a `Bash` command whose effect is a write is mutating. | `test_plan_mode.py::test_no_mutation_tool_succeeds_in_plan_mode` — attempt Edit, Write and `rm` under plan mode; assert every one is refused and the file on disk is unchanged; **plus `::test_mutation_via_shell_is_denied_in_plan_mode`** — a `Bash` write command is refused and the file is not created. | Plan mode is the user's guarantee that exploration is safe. Mitigation: the refusal is enforced in the permission engine against the **operation class**, so no tool implementation — and no shell command that writes — can bypass it. **A tool-name filter alone would not be sufficient**; see S4-PLAN-016. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S4-PLAN-016 | **ORIGINAL DESIGN DECISION**, derived from `claude-code-evidence.md` §9.4.1–§9.4.2 and `interface-spec.md` §5.8 | **Operation classification is a first-class layer.** Every tool call is classified into an operation class (`read` / `mutate_local` / `execute` / `network` / `process_control` / `irreversible`) from the *normalised* input, independently of the tool that requested it. Mode constraints and capability denials are evaluated against the operation class, so a capability with several routes (write via `Write`, via `Edit`, via `Bash`, via a plugin, via a subagent, via an MCP server) is denied on **all** routes, not on the one whose tool name was denied. | S4-PLAN-001, S6-MODE-020, S6-MODE-021 | `permissions/operations.py :: classify(normalised_input) -> OperationClass`; `permissions/engine.py :: decide(request, mode)` consumes the class, not the tool name; shell front-ends supply the effect classification for compound commands (interface-spec §5.8, §8). | `test_capability_parity.py::test_denying_one_route_denies_the_capability` — for every capability with more than one route, denying one route denies the capability; `test_plan_mode.py::test_plan_denial_names_the_operation_not_the_tool` — the message names the operation class and the normalised target, not just the tool. | A tool-level filter is a steering device, not a boundary: the reference's plan mode was observed to be routed around via the shell (O6), and the two explanations — enforcement gap vs model routing — are indistinguishable by observation. Mitigation: classify by **effect**, and treat an indeterminate command as mutating. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S4-PLAN-002 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-2; area3-planning.md F3-2 | Leaving plan mode is a **three-way** user choice — proceed with the plan, proceed with edits restricted to the plan, or keep planning — mediated by a dedicated plan-exit tool rather than by a keystroke. | S4-PLAN-001, S4-PLAN-003 | `tools/planexit.py :: exit_plan_mode(choice: Literal['accept','accept-edits','keep-planning'], plan: str) -> PlanExitResult`; `permissions/modes.py :: transition(from='plan', choice) -> Mode`. | `test_plan_exit.py::test_three_choices_produce_three_distinct_transitions` — assert the resulting mode and whether the plan file is written. | A two-way choice would force the user to reject a plan they want, in order to refine it. Mitigation: the three options are explicit and each maps to a documented mode transition. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S4-PLAN-003 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-3; area3-planning.md F3-3 | An approved plan is written to a Markdown artifact on disk under the plans directory with a machine-generated slug filename, and the artifact's path is reported back to the model. | S4-PLAN-002 | `plans/store.py :: write_plan(text, session_id) -> PlanRef(path, slug)` deriving a slug from the plan text and a session-scoped directory; `plans/store.py :: read_plan(path) -> str`. | `test_plan_store.py::test_slug_is_deterministic_and_filesystem_safe` and `::test_plan_path_is_reported` — assert the slug contains no path separator and that two runs of the same text give the same slug. | A slug derived from arbitrary model text can contain path separators and escape the plans directory. Mitigation: the slug generator is allow-listed to `[a-z0-9-]` and the final path is re-validated to be inside the plans root. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S4-PLAN-004 | agent-evidence.md §6 L10; agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-3 | The plan artifact is written **outside** the working tree (under the configuration root), so that creating a plan never perturbs the user's repository or appears in `git status`. | S4-PLAN-003 | `plans/store.py :: plans_root() -> Path` resolving under the config root rather than the project directory; the path is asserted at write time. | `test_plan_store.py::test_plan_not_inside_project` — assert the written path is not under `cwd` and that `git status --porcelain` is empty afterwards. | Writing into the repository would dirty the user's tree and could be committed accidentally. Mitigation: the plan root is a sibling of the projects root and is asserted to be outside the working tree. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S4-TASK-005 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-4; area3-planning.md F3-4 | Task decomposition is a **directed graph** with dependency edges, not a flat list: a task may declare tasks it depends on, and a task whose dependency is not complete is not eligible to start. | S4-TASK-006 | `tasks/graph.py :: TaskGraph` with `Task(id, subject, status, depends_on: list[str])`; `tasks/graph.py :: eligible_tasks() -> list[Task]` returning only tasks whose dependencies are all complete; `tasks/graph.py :: detect_cycles() -> list[list[str]]`. | `test_task_graph.py::test_dependent_task_not_eligible` and `::test_cycle_is_detected_and_rejected`. | A cycle would deadlock the run with no error. Mitigation: cycle detection at insertion, with the offending ids reported. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S4-TASK-006 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-5, F3-12; area3-planning.md F3-5, F3-12 | The task list is a model-driven checklist with per-item status (pending, in progress, complete), and a failure is **recorded in the task list** rather than only narrated in prose. | S4-TASK-005 | `tasks/store.py :: TaskStore` with `upsert(task)`, `set_status(id, status)`, `set_error(id, message)`; `tasks/store.py :: TaskStatus` enum including `PENDING/IN_PROGRESS/COMPLETE/FAILED`. | `test_tasks.py::test_failure_is_recorded_on_the_task` — fail a task and assert the stored status is `FAILED` and the error message is retained. | Failures that exist only in prose are lost when the context is compacted. Mitigation: the task store is the durable record and survives compaction (see S11). | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S4-TASK-007 | area-interactive-mode/_section-interactive-mode.md §2 F-I17; agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-5 | The task list renders as a toggled, compact widget showing at most five items at a time; with no items the toggle has no visible effect; the expanded/collapsed state is restored when a session with tasks is resumed, and starts collapsed when the list is empty. | S4-TASK-006, S1-SESSION-024 | `ui/tasklist.py :: TaskListWidget(max_visible=5)` with `toggle()` and `expanded_state` persisted on the session record. | `test_tasklist.py::test_five_item_cap` and `::test_empty_list_toggle_is_noop` and `::test_expanded_state_restored_on_resume`. | The widget is a view, not the store; capping the view must not truncate the stored task list. Mitigation: the cap is applied in the renderer only; the store keeps every task. | P1 extensibility / Phase 3 |
| S4-TASK-008 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-6; area3-planning.md F3-6 | The task-tracking tools are gated by model capability and are absent for models that do not support them; the user can override the gate, and an unrecognised model id does not get the tools by default. | S13-MODEL-030, S1-TOOLSET-022 | `capabilities/tasks.py :: task_tools_available(model) -> bool`; `capabilities/tasks.py :: TASK_TOOL_OVERRIDE_ENV = 'CLAUDE_CODE_ENABLE_TODO_TOOLS'`; the registry filters the task tools out when the gate is closed. | `test_task_gate.py::test_tools_absent_for_ungated_model` and `::test_env_override_enables_tools` and `::test_unrecognised_model_is_gated`. | Advertising tools the model cannot use wastes turns. Mitigation: the gate is applied at registry-filter time, alongside the platform gate. | P1 extensibility / Phase 3 |
| S4-TASK-009 | area-interactive-mode/_section-interactive-mode.md §2 F-I17 | The task list persists across context compactions, and a named, shareable task list can be selected through an environment variable that maps to a directory under the tasks root. | S4-TASK-006, S11-COMPACT-004 | `tasks/store.py :: TaskStore` rooted at `tasks_root() / name`; `tasks/store.py :: TASK_LIST_ID_ENV = 'CLAUDE_CODE_TASK_LIST_ID'`. | `test_tasks.py::test_tasks_survive_compaction` and `::test_named_task_list_directory`. | A shared task list is a cross-session channel and may be written by another session. Mitigation: the named directory is under the config root with the same ownership checks as other shared stores. | P2 safety-tier / Phase 3 |
| S4-LOOP-010 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-11; area3-planning.md F3-11 | The agent's iterative loop is a documented cycle — gather context, take action, verify results — and the harness does not itself decide when the task is complete; the model signals completion. | S4-TASK-006, S7-REPAIR-012 | `runtime/loop.py :: TurnLoop.run(turn) -> TurnOutcome` with phases `GATHER/ACT/VERIFY` recorded on the turn; `runtime/loop.py :: TurnOutcome(status=Literal['complete','interrupted','error','limit'])`, `status='complete'` set only by the model. | `test_loop.py::test_completion_is_model_signalled` and `::test_loop_records_phase_transitions`. | A harness-side completion heuristic can end a turn before the work is done. Mitigation: completion is signalled only by the model, and the harness records the phase sequence for audit. | P0 parity-core / Phase 1 |
| S4-LOOP-011 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 1 F10; area6-tests-errors.md §7b.3 H10 | The turn loop terminates on explicit limits — a maximum turn count, a maximum budget, an interrupt, or an error — and each termination reason is reported distinctly in the result envelope. | S1-RESULT-011, S4-LOOP-010 | `runtime/limits.py :: Limits(max_turns=None, max_budget_usd=None)`; `runtime/loop.py` raising `TurnLimitExceeded(kind)` with `kind=Literal['max_turns','max_budget']`. | `test_loop_limits.py::test_max_turns_terminates_with_distinct_reason` and `::test_max_budget_terminates_with_distinct_reason` and `::test_terminal_reason_in_envelope`. | Silent truncation of a long run makes a pipeline believe the work finished. Mitigation: every limit path sets an explicit terminal reason on the envelope. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S4-PLAN-012 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-14; area3-planning.md F3-14 | Continuation beyond a single turn is available through several distinct mechanisms — a goal-driven continuation, dynamic workflows, a batch runner, and agent teams — and they are separate surfaces, not one multiplexed command. | S10-TEAM-024, S10-WORKFLOW-026 | `commands/registry.py :: CONTINUATION_COMMANDS = ('goal','workflows','batch')`; each resolves to a distinct handler class implementing `CommandHandler.handle(ctx, args) -> CommandResult`. | `test_commands.py::test_continuation_commands_are_distinct_handlers` — assert each maps to a unique handler type. | Conflating them would make one mechanism's failure modes another's. Mitigation: distinct handler classes with distinct tests. | P1 extensibility / Phase 4 |
| S4-PLAN-013 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-15; area3-planning.md F3-15 | The workflow progress view is a keyboard-driven, resumable monitor: it shows per-step status and can be re-entered to resume a suspended run. | S10-WORKFLOW-026 | `ui/workflows.py :: WorkflowMonitor` with `render(state) -> Frame` and `handle_key(key) -> Action`; `workflows/engine.py :: WorkflowState` persisted to a journal. | `test_workflow_monitor.py::test_monitor_is_resumable` and `::test_step_status_rendered`. | A monitor that is not resumable forces a restart and loses progress. Mitigation: workflow state is journalled (see S10) and the monitor reads the journal, not live memory. | P1 extensibility / Phase 4 |
| S4-PLAN-014 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-16; area3-planning.md F3-16 | Decomposition prompt shapes are provided as reusable, named templates that the model can be pointed at, rather than leaving the decomposition quality to chance. | S4-TASK-005 | `prompts/templates.py :: DECOMPOSITION_TEMPLATES: dict[str, str]`; `prompts/templates.py :: render(name, **vars) -> str`. | `test_templates.py::test_every_template_renders` and `::test_template_names_are_stable`. | Template prose is a product asset and must not be treated as observed reference behaviour. Mitigation: this row's templates are original; only the *existence* of named reusable decomposition shapes is observed. | P2 safety-tier / Phase 3 |
| S4-PLAN-015 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-1, §4 Area 8 §2.1; area3-planning.md F3-1 | In plan mode the read-only tools remain available, and the set of available tools is reduced accordingly and published in the init event. | S4-PLAN-001, S1-TOOLSET-022 | `permissions/modes.py :: tools_for_mode(mode) -> set[str]`; the registry filter composes mode, platform, model capability and rule filters. | `test_plan_mode.py::test_read_tools_available_in_plan_mode` and `::test_mutation_tools_absent`. | A tool that is listed but always refused wastes a turn per attempt. Mitigation: the published set and the enforcement set are the same object. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S4-PLAN-016 | agent-evidence.md §6 L10, §4 Area 3 F3-2; area3-planning.md F3-3 | The plan artifact is written only on approval; a plan that is rejected or that keeps planning open leaves no artifact behind. | S4-PLAN-003, S4-PLAN-002 | `plans/store.py :: write_plan` called only from the plan-exit handler on an accepting choice; no other call site exists. | `test_plan_store.py::test_no_artifact_when_plan_rejected` and `::test_artifact_written_once_on_approval` — count files in the plans directory before and after. | Stray plan files accumulate in the config root. Mitigation: the write path has exactly one call site, asserted by a test that counts artifacts. | P1 extensibility / Phase 2 |
| S4-EXEC-017 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-13; area3-planning.md F3-13 | A delegated agent's result returns to the parent as a **summary only**, and the returned text is scanned for instruction-shaped content before it is placed in the parent's context. | S10-AGENT-020, S12-UX-020 | `agents/result.py :: sanitize_report(text) -> SanitizedReport` with `SanitizedReport(text, flags=[PromptInjectionFlag(kind, excerpt)])`; the flag list is surfaced to the parent, not silently dropped. | `test_agent_report.py::test_report_is_summarised_not_raw` and `::test_instruction_shaped_content_is_flagged` — feed a report containing an instruction-shaped line and assert a flag is raised. | A subagent that read hostile files can relay an injection to the parent. Mitigation: the report is scanned and the findings are shown to the parent and the operator. | P2 safety-tier / Phase 4 |
| S4-EXEC-018 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-8; area3-planning.md F3-8 | Built-in subagent types are a fixed, documented set; the read-only types (`Explore`, `Plan`) are read-only and one-shot, and their tool allowlist is enforced rather than advisory. | S10-AGENT-008 | `agents/types.py :: BUILTIN_AGENT_TYPES: dict[str, AgentTypeSpec]` with `AgentTypeSpec(name, tools, one_shot, read_only)`; `agents/runner.py` filtering the tool set by `spec.tools` before dispatch. | `test_agent_types.py::test_readonly_types_have_no_mutating_tools` and `::test_one_shot_agent_cannot_continue`. | An advisory allowlist would be defeated by a mis-routed tool call. Mitigation: the allowlist is applied by the dispatcher, not by the model. | P0 parity-core / Phase 4 |
| S4-EXEC-019 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-9; area3-planning.md F3-9 | Concurrency and nesting of delegated agents are bounded, and hitting a bound produces a documented, distinguishable failure signal rather than a silent truncation. | S10-AGENT-014 | `agents/limits.py :: AgentLimits(max_concurrent, max_depth)`; `agents/runner.py` raising `AgentLimitExceeded(kind=Literal['concurrency','depth','budget'])`. | `test_agent_limits.py::test_depth_limit_signalled` and `::test_concurrency_limit_signalled` and `::test_budget_refusal_signalled`. | Unbounded fan-out can exhaust the account's rate limit and the operator's budget. Mitigation: three separate bound kinds, each with its own test. | P0 parity-core / Phase 4 |
| S4-EXEC-020 | ORIGINAL DESIGN DECISION (the reference's plan-to-execution handoff was observed only indirectly through the plan artifact and plan-exit tool in agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-2/F3-3) | Accepting a plan seeds the task graph with one task per plan step, preserving the plan's ordering as dependency edges, so that execution cannot begin on a step the plan ordered later. | S4-TASK-005, S4-PLAN-002 | `tasks/graph.py :: seed_from_plan(steps: list[str]) -> TaskGraph` building a chain of `depends_on` edges; `tasks/graph.py :: TaskGraph.seed_from_plan`. | `test_plan_handoff.py::test_plan_steps_become_ordered_tasks` and `::test_later_step_not_eligible_before_earlier_completes`. | Seeding tasks from an unvalidated plan could import a step the operator never approved. Mitigation: only the plan text the operator approved is used, and the plan file is the sole source. | P1 extensibility / Phase 4 |
| S4-EXEC-021 | ORIGINAL DESIGN DECISION (derived from the observed `plan` mode semantics in agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-1 and the observed plan artifact behaviour in F3-3) | In plan mode, a mutating action is refused with a message that names the mutating operation and states that the plan mode is active, so the model can switch modes rather than retry blindly. | S4-PLAN-001, S7-DENIAL-010 | `permissions/engine.py :: deny(reason='plan_mode', operation, target) -> Decision` producing a model-facing reason string with a stable shape. | `test_plan_mode.py::test_refusal_names_operation_and_mode` — assert the denial text contains the operation name and the mode name. | A denial that does not explain itself causes repeated identical attempts. Mitigation: the reason string shape is asserted. | P1 extensibility / Phase 2 |
| S4-EXEC-022 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 3 F3-4, F3-12; area3-planning.md F3-4 | A task that fails is retained in the task list with its failure recorded, and its dependents are marked blocked rather than removed, so the graph still explains why work did not proceed. | S4-TASK-005, S4-TASK-006 | `tasks/graph.py :: TaskGraph.mark_failed(id, error) -> list[Task]` marking transitive dependents `BLOCKED`; `tasks/store.py :: TaskStatus.BLOCKED`. | `test_task_graph.py::test_failed_task_blocks_dependents` and `::test_failed_task_retained_in_list`. | Silently dropping a failed task hides the reason the work stopped. Mitigation: the graph keeps the node and records a blocked state. | P1 extensibility / Phase 4 |

## S5 — Controlled terminal execution

**Priority band:** P0 parity-core.

**Evidence base.** `agent-evidence.md` §4 Area 5 (F1–F24) and §5.8 (output-size handling table); `agent-reference-shots/areas/area5-bash-permissions.md` (per-finding detail); `agent-evidence.md` §6 L1/L2/L3/L13 (output and matching limitations); `agent-reference-shots/tool-result-dual-representation.json` and `tool-error-contract.json` (observed tool-result shapes); `docs-cache/tools-reference.md` (bash and PowerShell tool contracts); `docs-cache/env-vars.md` (`BASH_MAX_OUTPUT_LENGTH`).

**Scope note.** S5 owns *how a command is shaped, split, matched, executed, and how its output is sized*. S6 owns *whether it may run*. The boundary is: S5 produces the command model and the result model; S6 answers "allowed?".
| ID | Evidence | Acceptance criterion (testable) | Depends on | Proposed module + interface shape | Automated verification test | Security / operational risk + mitigation | Priority / Phase |
| :- | :- | :- | :- | :- | :- | :- | :- |
| S5-CMD-001 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F1; area5-bash-permissions.md F1 | The shell tool's input contract is a small set of named fields — a command string, a description, an optional timeout, an optional working directory, and a sandbox flag — and no other field is accepted. | none | `tools/shell.py :: ShellRequest(command: str, description: str, timeout: int/None, cwd: str/None, sandbox: bool/None)` as a strict schema; `tools/shell.py :: run(request) -> ShellOutcome`. | `test_shell_contract.py::test_unknown_field_rejected` and `::test_required_fields_present` — assert schema validation rejects an extra key. | Extra fields that are silently accepted hide typos in a safety-relevant input. Mitigation: strict schema with no unknown-key tolerance. | P0 parity-core / Phase 1 |
| S5-CMD-002 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 6 F1, F1b; area6-tests-errors.md F1 | A successful command returns a **typed** payload with `stdout`, `stderr`, `interrupted`, `isImage` and `noOutputExpected`; a failed command returns an `Error: `-prefixed **string** instead. The two representations are never mixed in one result. | S7-RESULT-004 | `tools/shell.py :: ShellOutcome` (dataclass) and `tools/shell.py :: ShellFailure(message: str)`; `protocol/toolresult.py :: encode(outcome) -> dict / str` choosing the representation. | `test_tool_result.py::test_success_is_object_and_failure_is_string` — assert the JSON shape of each, and that the failure string starts with `Error: `. | A single polymorphic shape invites a consumer to forget the string case. Mitigation: the encoder is a single function with a test pinning both shapes. | P0 parity-core / Phase 1 |
| S5-CMD-003 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 6 F1b; area6-tests-errors.md F1b | The child process's stderr is merged into the `stdout` field of the result; the `stderr` field of the result carries the **harness's** own diagnostics, not the child's. | S5-CMD-002 | `tools/shell.py :: ShellOutcome.stdout` holds combined output; `tools/shell.py :: harness_stderr` is a separate channel collected by the runner. | `test_tool_result.py::test_child_stderr_appears_in_stdout` — run a command writing to both streams and assert both texts are in `stdout` and that `stderr` is empty. | Confusing the two channels makes it impossible to tell a command's diagnostics from the harness's. Mitigation: the runner keeps the channels separate at the capture layer. | P0 parity-core / Phase 1 |
| S5-CMD-004 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 6 F2; area6-tests-errors.md F2 | The exit code is a **prepended line** in the model-facing text (`Exit code N`), not a separate field in the tool result. | S5-CMD-002 | `tools/shell.py :: format_exit(code: int) -> str` returning `f'Exit code {code}\n'`; the model-facing text is `exit_line + payload`. | `test_tool_result.py::test_exit_code_is_a_prepended_line` — assert the text begins with `Exit code 1\n` and that no `exit_code` key exists in the payload. | A prepended line is machine-readable but easy to miss in a long output. Mitigation: the prefix is always the first characters of the model-facing text. | P0 parity-core / Phase 1 |
| S5-CMD-005 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 6 F3, §5.8; area6-tests-errors.md F3 | Exit code 1 is treated as a **success** for a fixed allowlist of commands (`grep`, `rg`, `egrep`, `fgrep`, `find`, `diff`, `test`, `[`, `git diff`, `git grep`) and as a failure for every other command. | S5-CMD-004 | `tools/shell.py :: BENIGN_EXIT1_COMMANDS: frozenset[str]`; `tools/shell.py :: is_benign_failure(command, exit_code) -> bool` matching on the command's first token. | `test_exit_codes.py::test_benign_list_success` and `::test_non_benign_exit1_is_failure` — parameterise over the whole list and a control. | Treating a failed `git diff` as success hides a real error; treating `grep`'s no-match as a failure causes endless repair loops. Mitigation: the allowlist is an explicit constant with a boundary test. | P0 parity-core / Phase 1 |
| S5-OUTPUT-006 | agent-evidence.md §5.8, §4 Area 6 F6; area5-bash-permissions.md F4 | Successful command output above roughly 30,000 characters is not truncated inline: it is persisted to a file under the session directory and the model-facing text is replaced by a wrapper that names the file path and includes a 2,000-character preview. | S5-OUTPUT-008, S7-RESULT-004 | `output/persist.py :: persist_output(text, session_dir, inline_limit=30_000, preview_chars=2_000) -> PersistedRef`; the wrapper text names the path and embeds the preview. | `test_output_ladder.py::test_success_overflow_persists_with_path` — assert the returned text contains the persisted path and that the path exists with the full content. | The persisted path is a file the model will later read; it must be inside the session directory and not in the project. Mitigation: persisted files live under the session dir with a reserved prefix. | P0 parity-core / Phase 1 |
| S5-OUTPUT-007 | agent-evidence.md §5.8, §6 L1; area6-tests-errors.md F7 | Failed command output above roughly 10,000 characters is reduced to a head-and-tail excerpt **with no file path**, so the full failure output is not retrievable. This is a known limitation of the reference and is reproduced deliberately. | S5-OUTPUT-006 | `output/truncate.py :: excerpt(text, head_chars, tail_chars) -> str` with `FAILURE_INLINE_LIMIT = 10_000`; the failure path calls `excerpt` and never `persist_output`. | `test_output_ladder.py::test_failure_overflow_has_no_file_path` — assert the text contains no session-directory path and contains both a head and a tail marker. | Reproducing the limitation is a parity decision; improving on it is a divergence that must be a documented, deliberate choice. Mitigation: the behaviour is pinned by a test so any improvement is an explicit change. | P0 parity-core / Phase 1 |
| S5-OUTPUT-008 | agent-evidence.md §5.8; docs-cache/env-vars.md (`BASH_MAX_OUTPUT_LENGTH`) | The inline success limit is configurable through `BASH_MAX_OUTPUT_LENGTH` with a default of 30,000 and a ceiling of 150,000; a separate `bashOutputMaxChars` setting is capped at 128,000. A value above the ceiling is clamped, not honoured. | S5-OUTPUT-006 | `config/env.py :: BASH_MAX_OUTPUT_LENGTH` spec with `default=30_000`, `max=150_000`; `config/settings.py :: bashOutputMaxChars` with `max=128_000`. | `test_output_limits.py::test_env_var_clamps_to_ceiling` and `::test_setting_clamps_to_ceiling`. | An unbounded limit lets a single command consume the whole context window. Mitigation: two independent ceilings, both clamped. | P1 extensibility / Phase 2 |
| S5-CMD-010 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F8; area5-bash-permissions.md F8 | Permission rules for a command have the shape `Tool(specifier)`, and evaluation is deny → ask → allow with **first match wins**; there is no specificity sorting, so a broad allow does not override an earlier narrow deny. | S6-RULE-012 | `permissions/engine.py :: evaluate(rules, tool, specifier) -> Decision` iterating the rule list in order and returning on the first match; `permissions/rules.py :: Rule(tool, kind, specifier)`. | `test_permission_eval.py::test_first_match_wins_no_specificity_sorting` — order rules broad-allow-first and narrow-deny-second and assert the deny does not fire; then reverse and assert it does. | Specificity sorting would make a broad allow silently outrank a narrow deny, which is the classic permission bypass. Mitigation: strict first-match ordering, pinned by a test. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S5-CMD-011 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F9, §6 L2; area5-bash-permissions.md F9 | Bash rule wildcards are matched against the command **as written**; a rule that matches the literal text does not match a semantically equivalent but differently written command, and vice versa. | S5-CMD-010 | `permissions/glob.py :: match_bash(pattern, command) -> bool` operating on the raw command string with the documented wildcard semantics. | `test_bash_matching.py::test_rule_matches_command_as_written` — assert a rule that does not literally match the command does not fire. | Literal matching is conservative (fails closed towards prompting). Mitigation: the conservative behaviour is the specification, not a bug to be 'fixed'. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S5-CMD-012 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F10; area5-bash-permissions.md F10 | A compound command is split into its constituent operations and **each** must be independently allowed; the whole command is refused if any part is not allowed. | S5-CMD-010, S5-CMD-014 | `shell/parse.py :: split_operations(command) -> list[Operation]` with `Operation(text, kind=Literal['simple','pipeline','redirect','compound'])`; `permissions/engine.py` evaluates every operation. | `test_compound.py::test_each_subcommand_evaluated` and `::test_disallowed_part_refuses_whole`. | Evaluating only the first part of `safe && dangerous` is a bypass. Mitigation: the splitter is the single source of operations and every operation is evaluated. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S5-CMD-013 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F11; area5-bash-permissions.md F11 | Wrapper commands and environment-variable assignments are stripped from the command before rule matching, so `FOO=1 git status` is matched as `git status`. | S5-CMD-012 | `shell/parse.py :: strip_wrappers(command) -> str` removing leading `VAR=value` assignments and known wrappers (`env`, `nohup`, `time`, `command`, `sudo`); `shell/parse.py :: strip_wrappers` used before matching. | `test_wrapper_strip.py::test_env_prefix_stripped` and `::test_wrapper_stripped`. | Over-stripping could change the command's meaning; under-stripping causes a rule to miss. Mitigation: the stripper has an explicit, tested list of wrappers and a conservative default (do not strip unknown prefixes). | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S5-CMD-014 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F12; area5-bash-permissions.md F12 | A built-in read-only command set runs without a prompt, with a documented list of exceptions where the same command does require approval. | S6-RULE-012 | `permissions/readonly.py :: READONLY_COMMANDS` with `READONLY_EXCEPTIONS`; `permissions/engine.py` consulting it before the rule list. | `test_readonly.py::test_readonly_set_runs_without_prompt` and `::test_exception_requires_approval` — parameterise over the set and each exception. | A read-only allowlist that includes a mutating variant would run a mutation unattended. Mitigation: exceptions are enumerated explicitly and tested. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S5-BG-015 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F5, F6, F7; area5-bash-permissions.md F5–F7 | A foreground command that exceeds its timeout is auto-promoted to the background rather than being killed, and the promotion is reported to the model. | S5-BG-016, S7-RESULT-004 | `tools/shell.py :: run(request)` spawning via `exec/shell.py :: spawn(command, timeout) -> ProcessHandle`; on timeout the runner calls `handle.promote_to_background()` and returns a background outcome carrying the handle id. | `test_background.py::test_timeout_promotes_to_background` — run a 60 s sleep with a 1 s timeout and assert the outcome is a background handle, not a kill. | A killed command can leave a half-written file or a running server. Mitigation: promotion, not termination, is the default; the model is told the command is still running. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S5-BG-016 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F6; area5-bash-permissions.md F6 | Background execution has a three-party contract: the harness can read the command's output while it runs, the model can poll it, and the output is delivered into the conversation when the command finishes. | S5-BG-015, S12-UX-010 | `exec/background.py :: BackgroundJob(id, output_path, state_path)` with `poll() -> JobStatus`, `read_output() -> str`, `stop() -> None`; `exec/background.py :: JobStatus` enum (`running`, `exited`, `failed`, `killed`). | `test_background.py::test_output_readable_while_running` and `::test_completion_delivers_output`. | Background jobs outlive the turn and can keep running after the session ends. Mitigation: jobs are recorded in a job registry with a lifetime bound and a stop notice (S5-BG-018). | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S5-BG-017 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F7; area5-bash-permissions.md F7 | Background commands in an unattended session have a time limit and, when stopped, produce a fixed, recognisable stop notice rather than silence. | S5-BG-016, S1-SESSION-019 | `exec/background.py :: BackgroundJob` with `max_runtime_s` and a `stop_notice` constant; the job writes the notice to its output file on stop. | `test_background.py::test_unattended_job_times_out_with_notice` — run with a short limit and assert the notice text is present in the output. | A silent stop looks like a hang. Mitigation: the notice is a constant, asserted by a test. | P1 extensibility / Phase 2 |
| S5-BG-018 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 1 F12; area6-tests-errors.md §7b.3 H14 | At process exit, the harness waits for background work up to a documented ceiling (10 minutes by default) and the ceiling is overridable by an environment variable. | S5-BG-016, S1-SESSION-023 | `runtime/exit.py :: await_background(jobs, ceiling_s=600)`; the ceiling read from a documented environment variable at exit time. | `test_exit.py::test_exit_waits_for_background_up_to_ceiling` — assert the process waits for a short job and exits promptly. | An unbounded wait makes CI hang. Mitigation: a hard ceiling, overridable but always finite. | P1 extensibility / Phase 2 |
| S5-PS-019 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F20; area5-bash-permissions.md F20 | The PowerShell tool mirrors the shell tool but matches rules against the **PowerShell AST**, and canonicalises command aliases before matching, so `?` and `where` are treated as their full names. | S5-CMD-010, S3-READ-010 | `shell/powershell.py :: parse_powershell(command) -> PsCommand` using the PowerShell parser; `shell/powershell.py :: canonicalize(command) -> str` expanding aliases via a static alias table. | `test_powershell.py::test_alias_canonicalised_before_matching` and `::test_ast_used_not_regex` — assert a rule matching the canonical form fires for an alias invocation. | Regex matching of PowerShell is famously bypassable. Mitigation: the AST is the matching input; the alias table is a static, versioned constant. | P1 extensibility / Phase 3 |
| S5-MON-020 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F21; area5-bash-permissions.md F21 | The Monitor tool is a distinct execution mode: it watches a long-running process and **streams** matching lines to the caller as they appear, rather than returning at exit. | S5-BG-016 | `tools/monitor.py :: monitor(command, pattern, timeout) -> MonitorStream` yielding `MonitorEvent(line, matched, at)`; `tools/monitor.py :: MonitorStream.close()`. | `test_monitor.py::test_lines_streamed_as_produced` — assert an event is delivered before the process exits; `::test_non_matching_lines_filtered`. | A streaming tool that buffers defeats its purpose and can exhaust memory. Mitigation: incremental line delivery with a bounded buffer. | P1 extensibility / Phase 3 |
| S5-EDIT-021 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F22; area5-bash-permissions.md F22 | In `acceptEdits` mode, a specific, enumerated set of filesystem commands is auto-approved (`mkdir`, `touch`, `rm`, `rmdir`, `mv`, `cp`, `sed`) — and nothing else. | S6-MODE-020, S5-CMD-014 | `permissions/modes.py :: ACCEPT_EDITS_COMMANDS: frozenset[str]`; the mode filter adds these to the auto-approved set. | `test_accept_edits.py::test_enumerated_commands_auto_approved` and `::test_unlisted_command_requires_approval`. | `rm` in the auto-approved set is a strong action; it is safe only because the critical-path circuit breaker (S6) still applies. Mitigation: the two controls are independent and both are tested. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S5-ENV-022 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F23; area5-bash-permissions.md F23 | Environment and state that persist between commands are defined explicitly (working-directory changes, exported variables, and the shell session identity), and each is observable in a later command. | S5-CMD-021 | `exec/session.py :: ShellSession` with `cwd`, `env_overlay: dict[str,str]`, `persistent: bool`; `exec/session.py :: apply_to_env()` building the child environment. | `test_shell_session.py::test_cd_persists` and `::test_exported_var_persists` — run two commands in one session and assert the second sees the first's state. | A shell session that silently resets can produce different results between the model's expectation and reality. Mitigation: the session state model is explicit and tested. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S5-WIN-023 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F24; area5-bash-permissions.md F24 | Windows shell behaviour is a distinct, documented surface: the default shell, the presence of Git Bash, the PowerShell path, and the differences in quoting and redirection are surfaced rather than assumed to be POSIX. | S5-PS-019, S1-CLI-006 | `exec/platform.py :: ShellFlavor` (`bash`, `powershell`, `cmd`) with `detect(platform) -> ShellFlavor`; the init event publishes `powershell_path`. | `test_windows_shell.py::test_shell_flavor_detection` and `::test_powershell_path_published_in_init`. | Assuming POSIX on Windows produces quoting bugs that can change the meaning of a command. Mitigation: the flavour is an explicit, published value. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S5-EXEC-024 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F6, §6 L3; area5-bash-permissions.md F6 | A command executes under a controlled working directory and environment, and the effective working directory of a backgrounded command is recorded so the model can reason about relative paths. | S5-ENV-022, S10-WORKTREE-028 | `exec/session.py :: ShellSession.effective_cwd`; `exec/background.py :: BackgroundJob.cwd` recorded in the job state file. | `test_exec.py::test_background_job_records_cwd` — assert the state file contains the working directory used. | A background command whose working directory is not recorded can act in an unexpected directory. Mitigation: the cwd is part of the job record and is surfaced to the model. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S5-EXEC-025 | ORIGINAL DESIGN DECISION (the reference's handling of a command killed by a signal was not directly characterised; the observed contract is the exit-code prefix described in agent-evidence.md §4 Area 6 F2) | A command terminated by a signal is reported with a distinct, non-numeric exit marker that names the signal, so the model can distinguish a crash from a normal non-zero exit. | S5-CMD-004 | `tools/shell.py :: ShellOutcome.exit_kind: Literal['code','signal','timeout']` with `signal_name` set for the signal case; the model-facing text uses a distinct marker. | `test_shell.py::test_signal_reported_distinctly` — terminate a child with a signal and assert the marker differs from `Exit code N`. | A crash presented as exit code 1 invites the model to 'fix' a non-existent bug. Mitigation: the exit kind is explicit. | P1 extensibility / Phase 3 |
| S5-EXEC-026 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F4, §5.8; area5-bash-permissions.md F4 | The output-overflow wrapper is a **parseable envelope** with a fixed structure (a marker line, the file path, and the preview), so a consumer can detect overflow without string guessing. | S5-OUTPUT-006 | `output/persist.py :: OverflowEnvelope(marker, path, preview)` with `to_text()` producing the fixed layout; `output/persist.py :: parse_overflow(text) -> Optional[OverflowEnvelope]`. | `test_output_ladder.py::test_overflow_envelope_round_trips` — assert `parse_overflow` recovers the path from the rendered text. | A free-form overflow notice cannot be parsed reliably. Mitigation: the envelope is structured and round-trip tested. | P0 parity-core / Phase 1 |
| S5-EXEC-027 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 6 F6, §5.8; area5-bash-permissions.md F4 | A hard ceiling of 5 GB applies to a single command's captured output; exceeding it kills the command rather than growing without limit. | S5-OUTPUT-006, S5-OUTPUT-007 | `output/persist.py :: HARD_OUTPUT_LIMIT = 5 * 1024**3`; the capture layer aborts and kills the child when the limit is reached. | `test_output_hard_limit.py::test_capture_aborts_at_hard_limit` — simulate the limit and assert the child is terminated and an error is produced. | The hard limit is the last line of defence against a runaway producer. Mitigation: the limit is enforced in the capture layer, below the persistence layer. | P2 safety-tier / Phase 2 |
| S5-EXEC-028 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F8, §4 Area 8 §3.2; area5-bash-permissions.md F9 | Bash wildcard semantics are documented with worked examples, including the difference between `Bash(rm *)` and `Bash(rm -rf *)`, and the trailing-`*` behaviour that matches a prefix. | S5-CMD-011 | `permissions/glob.py :: match_bash` with the documented wildcard rules and a table of worked examples used as the test corpus. | `test_bash_matching.py::test_worked_examples_from_contract` — table-driven over the documented examples. | A wildcard that matches more than intended is a permission bypass; one that matches less causes nuisance prompts. Mitigation: the worked examples are the test corpus and are part of the contract. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S5-EXEC-029 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 6 F1, §4 Area 5 F2; area6-tests-errors.md F1 | The model-facing text of a tool result omits the `Error: ` prefix that the machine-facing result carries, so the model reads a natural-language failure while automation can still match the marker. | S5-CMD-002, S7-RESULT-004 | `protocol/toolresult.py :: to_model_text(payload) -> str` stripping a leading `Error: `; `protocol/toolresult.py :: to_machine_payload()` keeping it. | `test_tool_result.py::test_model_text_omits_error_prefix` and `::test_machine_payload_keeps_prefix`. | Two representations of one fact risk drift. Mitigation: one encoder, two projections, both tested. | P0 parity-core / Phase 1 |
| S5-EXEC-030 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F2, §4 Area 6 F9; area5-bash-permissions.md F2 | A command's failure text is specific and actionable: it names the offending fragment of the command and the reason, rather than reporting a bare 'permission denied'. | S6-DENIAL-010 | `tools/shell.py :: failure_reason(command, cause) -> str` producing a text that includes the offending fragment and a remedy hint; the string shape is asserted. | `test_failure_text.py::test_reason_names_offending_fragment`. | A vague reason causes the model to retry the same failing command. Mitigation: the reason template is a single function and is shape-tested. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |


---

## S6 — Permission modes, approvals, cancellation, recovery

**Priority band:** P0 parity-core. This subsystem carries the highest safety weight in the document; several rows here are P2 safety-tier despite the subsystem band, because the risk they mitigate is not a feature gap but a hazard.

**Evidence base.** `agent-evidence.md` §4 Area 8 (F-1 … F-22) and §5.4 (deny-form semantics table); `agent-reference-shots/areas/area8-permissions.md` (per-finding detail, §§2–9); `agent-evidence.md` §6 L2/L3/L7/L9/L18/L19/L23; `agent-reference-shots/tool-error-contract.json` (observed `system.permission_denied` event shape); `agent-reference-shots/areas/area5-bash-permissions.md` F13/F14/F16/F17/F19 (prompt layout, structured metadata, sandbox, protected paths, headless approvals); `agent-reference-shots/cli-reference/claude-help-full.txt` (`--permission-mode` value set, `--dangerously-skip-permissions`).

**Scope note.** S6 answers exactly one question for every tool call: *may this run, and who says so?* It also owns cancellation and the recovery affordances.
| ID | Evidence | Acceptance criterion (testable) | Depends on | Proposed module + interface shape | Automated verification test | Security / operational risk + mitigation | Priority / Phase |
| :- | :- | :- | :- | :- | :- | :- | :- |
| S6-MODE-001 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §2.1, F-4; area8-permissions.md §2.1–§2.2 | Exactly six permission modes exist with the documented semantics: `default` (UI label 'Manual'; reads only), `acceptEdits` (reads, file edits, and an enumerated set of filesystem commands), `plan` (reads plus classifier-approved commands), `auto` (everything with background safety checks), `dontAsk` (pre-approved tools and in-directory file reads; anything that would prompt is denied), `bypassPermissions` (everything). | none | `permissions/modes.py :: Mode` enum with the six members and a `label` property; `permissions/modes.py :: ModeSemantics` describing what each mode auto-approves. | `test_modes.py::test_exactly_six_modes` and `::test_mode_labels` — assert the enum members and their UI labels, and that `default` is labelled 'Manual'. | An extra mode expands the space of combinations the operator must reason about. Mitigation: the mode set is closed and asserted. | P0 parity-core / Phase 1 |
| S6-MODE-002 | area8-permissions.md §2.2; agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 F-4 | `manual` is accepted as an **alias** for `default` wherever the value is typed, and is normalised to `default` in the wire protocol: the init event's `permissionMode` reads `default` when `manual` was requested. | S6-MODE-001 | `permissions/modes.py :: normalise(value: str) -> Mode` mapping `manual` → `default`; the init event builder writes the normalised value. | `test_modes.py::test_manual_normalises_to_default` — assert the init event's `permissionMode` is `default` for `--permission-mode manual`. | Two spellings of the same mode must not create two permission states. Mitigation: normalisation is a single function applied at parse time. | P0 parity-core / Phase 1 |
| S6-MODE-003 | area8-permissions.md §2.2; agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 F-4 | The mode value is visible on the wire in the init event, and `--dangerously-skip-permissions` is reported as `bypassPermissions`. | S6-MODE-001, S1-CLI-006 | `protocol/events.py :: InitEvent.permissionMode: str`; `cli/flags.py` mapping the skip flag onto the mode before the event is built. | `test_modes.py::test_mode_is_published_in_init` — parameterise over the six values and assert the init event field. | Operators and monitoring tools depend on the true mode. Mitigation: the published value comes from the same normalised object the engine uses. | P0 parity-core / Phase 1 |
| S6-MODE-004 | area8-permissions.md §2.3; area-interactive-mode/_section-interactive-mode.md §2 F-I1 | `Shift+Tab` cycles the permission modes in the order auto → default → acceptEdits → plan, and from `auto` the first press returns to `default`; the cycle takes effect at the next tool call boundary, not retroactively. | S6-MODE-001, S1-INPUT-026 | `ui/footer.py :: cycle_mode(current: Mode) -> Mode` implementing the documented order; `permissions/engine.py :: mode_changes_apply_at_next_call_boundary` — the engine reads the mode from the session context at dispatch time. | `test_mode_cycle.py::test_cycle_order` and `::test_change_takes_effect_at_next_call_boundary` — change the mode during a turn and assert the in-flight call keeps the old mode and the next call uses the new one. | A mode change applied mid-call could allow an action under a weaker mode than the user believes is active. Mitigation: the mode is read per call, never cached across a call boundary. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-RULE-010 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §3.1, F-1; area8-permissions.md §3.1 | The permission rule set is evaluated in a fixed order — deny rules first, then ask rules, then allow rules — with the first matching rule deciding the outcome; rules are scope-independent (a rule in a project file and one in a user file are compared on the same terms). | S5-CMD-010 | `permissions/engine.py :: evaluate(rule_set, request) -> Decision` implementing `deny → ask → allow` with first-match-wins; `permissions/rules.py :: RuleSet` ordered by kind then by declaration order. | `test_permission_eval.py::test_deny_beats_ask_beats_allow` and `::test_scope_independent_evaluation` — place the same rule text in a project file and a user file and assert the outcome is the same. | Scope-independent evaluation prevents a project rule from being 'outranked' by a user rule in a confusing way, and vice versa. Mitigation: ordering is fixed and tested. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-RULE-011 | agent-evidence.md §5.4, §4 Area 8 F-2; area8-permissions.md §3.8 | A **bare-name** deny (e.g. `"Bash"`) removes the tool from the model's tool schema entirely — the model never sees it — while a **scoped** deny (e.g. `"Bash(rm *)"`, `"Write(/etc/**)"`, `"mcp__github__*"`) leaves the tool visible and blocks the call at dispatch time. | S6-RULE-010, S1-TOOLSET-022 | `permissions/engine.py :: apply_to_toolset(rules, tools) -> list[ToolSpec]` removing bare-denied tools; `permissions/engine.py :: decide()` raising a scoped-deny for scoped matches. | `test_deny_forms.py::test_bare_deny_removes_tool_from_schema` and `::test_scoped_deny_keeps_tool_and_blocks_call` — assert the published tool list and the dispatch outcome. | The two forms have very different footprints; conflating them either hides a capability the operator wanted or advertises a capability that will always be refused. Mitigation: the two mechanisms are separate code paths with separate tests. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-RULE-012 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 F-3, §4 Area 5 F8 | Permission rules are enforced by the harness, not by the model: a rule is applied to a tool call before dispatch regardless of what the model says about the action. | S6-RULE-010 | `permissions/engine.py :: decide(request) -> Decision` invoked in the tool-dispatch path before any tool implementation runs; the dispatcher has no bypass parameter. | `test_permission_enforcement.py::test_rule_applies_regardless_of_model_text` — include a model message asserting the action is allowed and assert the call is still refused. | A model-side honour system is not a control. Mitigation: enforcement is in the dispatcher, and the dispatcher exposes no skip path. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-RULE-013 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §3.3, F-8; area8-permissions.md §3.3 | Compound commands, wrapper invocations, and command shapes a rule does not match are documented explicitly, and a rule that does not match a compound command's *whole* text does not fire for it — the per-operation evaluation of S5-CMD-012 is the only thing that grants a compound command. | S5-CMD-012 | `permissions/glob.py :: match_bash` operating per operation; `permissions/rules.py` documenting the non-matching shapes. | `test_rule_matching.py::test_rule_not_matching_whole_compound_does_not_grant_it`. | Over-broad rule matching would let a narrow allow rule authorise a compound command. Mitigation: rules are evaluated per operation, and the test pins the conservative behaviour. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-RULE-014 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §3.4, §3.5, §3.6; area8-permissions.md §3.4–§3.6 | Path rules for Read/Edit distinguish project-relative, home-relative and absolute forms; symlinks are resolved before matching; network paths are treated distinctly from local paths. | S3-READ-001, S6-PATH-024 | `permissions/pathmatch.py :: match_path(rule_spec, path, cwd) -> bool` with a resolver that canonicalises symlinks first; `permissions/pathmatch.py :: PathForm` enum. | `test_path_rules.py::test_symlink_resolved_before_match` and `::test_network_path_distinguished`. | Failing to resolve symlinks lets a rule for one path be bypassed via a link to another. Mitigation: resolution happens before matching, and the test creates a real symlink. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-PROT-020 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §4.1, F-5; area8-permissions.md §4.1 | Writes to protected paths are never auto-approved. Protected directories include `.git`, `.config/git`, `.vscode`, `.idea`, `.husky`, `.cargo`, `.devcontainer`, `.yarn`, `.mvn`, `.claude` (with enumerated exceptions) and any plugin directory; protected files include `.gitconfig`, `.gitmodules`, shell rc files, `.npmrc`, `.pre-commit-config.yaml`, `.mcp.json`, `.claude.json` and the symlink target of a settings file. **Allow rules cannot pre-approve them** — the safety check runs before allow rules are evaluated. | S6-RULE-010, S6-CRIT-021 | `permissions/protected.py :: PROTECTED_DIRS`, `PROTECTED_FILES` with `PROTECTED_EXCEPTIONS`; `permissions/engine.py :: decide()` evaluating the protected-path check *before* the rule list. | `test_protected_paths.py::test_allow_rule_cannot_preapprove_protected_path` and `::test_protected_exceptions_allowed` — assert the refusal and the exception carve-outs. | The ordering (protected check before allow rules) is the whole control. Mitigation: the check is a separate, earlier stage in `decide()` and a test asserts the ordering explicitly. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-CRIT-021 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §4.2, F-5, F-6; area8-permissions.md §4.2 | A removal (`rm`/`rmdir`) targeting a **critical path** is refused regardless of allow rules and regardless of a `PreToolUse` hook returning `allow`. Critical paths are: the filesystem root, top-level directories, the home directory (including its Windows 8.3 short name), Windows drive roots and their top-level directories, the working directory and its parents, and additional working directories (when the removal is a glob under one). | S6-PROT-020 | `permissions/critical.py :: is_critical_path(target, ctx) -> bool`; `permissions/engine.py` evaluating the critical-path check before both rules and hooks. | `test_critical_paths.py::test_allow_rule_cannot_approve_critical_removal` and `::test_hook_allow_cannot_approve_critical_removal` and `::test_home_short_name_is_critical`. | This is the circuit breaker that guards against model error. Mitigation: it is checked before rules *and* before hooks, and the ordering is asserted. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-CRIT-022 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §4.2; area8-permissions.md §4.2 | The critical-path check understands variable expansion and command substitution rather than only literal paths. It flags: a glob or trailing slash directly under a shell variable, a positional-parameter form, a variable combined with a common top-level directory name, a variable assigned from a directory-printing substitution, a target that exists only as a command substitution, a trailing substitution after a critical path, a backslash-only target, a Windows volume-by-GUID path, and some `/*` or `/*/` targets. | S6-CRIT-021, S5-CMD-012 | `permissions/critical.py :: analyse_removal(command) -> CriticalAnalysis` with `CriticalAnalysis(is_critical, matched_form)`; the analyser handles `$VAR`, `$1`, `$(...)`, backticks, nested subshells, brace groups, process substitution and inline `sh -c` scripts. | `test_critical_paths.py::test_expansion_forms_flagged` — table-driven over the documented forms with the documented example commands. | The check must fail closed: an unresolvable target is treated as unverifiable rather than as safe. Mitigation: the analyser reports an explicit `unverifiable` state which is treated as critical. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-CRIT-023 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §4.2; area8-permissions.md §4.2 | The critical-path check inspects nested forms: subshells, brace groups, command substitution, process substitution, and inline scripts passed to `sh`/`bash`/`zsh` with `-c`. | S6-CRIT-022 | `permissions/critical.py :: analyse_removal` recursing into nested command structures; `shell/parse.py :: find_nested_scripts(command) -> list[str]`. | `test_critical_paths.py::test_nested_forms_inspected` — assert a removal hidden inside a subshell is still flagged. | A nested form is the obvious way to hide a critical removal from a literal check. Mitigation: recursion is required and tested for each nesting form. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-CRIT-024 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §4.2; area8-permissions.md §4.2 | In `auto` and `bypassPermissions` modes a critical-path removal is still put to the user, with a **two-minute** countdown in the terminal; elsewhere it is denied outright. After three unanswered countdowns in a session, the prompt stops being shown and further critical removals are denied immediately. | S6-CRIT-021, S6-APPROVE-026 | `permissions/engine.py :: critical_outcome(mode, terminal) -> Decision`; `ui/confirm.py :: CountdownPrompt(timeout_s=120)`; `session/state.py :: unanswered_countdown_count` incrementing on expiry. | `test_critical_paths.py::test_countdown_two_minutes` and `::test_three_unanswered_countdowns_switch_to_deny`. | An indefinite wait in an unattended run would stall; an immediate denial in an attended run would be hostile. Mitigation: the two-minute bound plus the escalating deny-after-three rule is tested at both boundaries. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-CRIT-025 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §4.6; area8-permissions.md §4.6 | A separate PowerShell/cmd removal check exists for Windows shells, and each safeguard has a documented environment-variable kill switch. | S6-CRIT-022, S6-PS-028 | `permissions/critical.py :: analyse_powershell_removal(command) -> CriticalAnalysis`; `config/env.py` with the per-safeguard kill-switch variables. | `test_critical_paths.py::test_powershell_removal_checked` and `::test_kill_switch_disables_safeguard`. | A kill switch that is silently ignored is a false sense of control. Mitigation: the switch is read at the check site and tested. | P1 extensibility / Phase 3 |
| S6-CLS-026 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §4.6, F-12, F-13; area8-permissions.md §4.6 | `auto` mode replaces the human prompt with a second, model-based classifier whose rule corpus is **data**: 17 allow rules, 72 soft-deny rules, 1 hard-deny rule, and a list of named environment slots. A soft-deny rule requires the user to have *named* the operation and its target; the corpus is inspectable as JSON. | S6-MODE-001 | `classifier/corpus.py :: AutoModeCorpus` loaded from a versioned JSON document with `allow`, `soft_deny`, `hard_deny`, `environment` keys; `classifier/rules.py :: Rule(name, kind, must_name)`. | `test_auto_classifier.py::test_corpus_shape` — assert the four top-level keys and that every soft-deny rule carries a `must name` clause; `::test_corpus_is_versioned_data_not_code`. | A classifier whose rules are hard-coded cannot be audited or updated safely. Mitigation: the corpus is a versioned data file that is validated on load and whose shape is asserted. | P1 extensibility / Phase 3 |
| S6-CLS-027 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 F-13; area8-permissions.md §4.6 (observed denial text) | A classifier refusal names the **specific rule** that fired and instructs the agent to complete the rest of the task and then stop and explain to the user, rather than failing generically. | S6-CLS-026, S6-DENIAL-010 | `classifier/engine.py :: review(action) -> Classification` with `Classification(outcome=Literal['allow','soft_deny','hard_deny'], rule_name)`; `permissions/engine.py` rendering the refusal with the rule name. | `test_auto_classifier.py::test_refusal_names_rule_and_stop_instruction` — assert the denial text contains the rule name and the stop-and-explain instruction. | A named rule makes the refusal auditable and lets the user see why. Mitigation: the refusal template always includes the rule name; a test asserts it. | P1 extensibility / Phase 3 |
| S6-CLS-028 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §4.6 (OBSERVED-CLI); area8-permissions.md §4.6 | A classifier refusal can be overridden by a server-side policy layer that operates independently of the local permission engine — the local mode may read `bypassPermissions` while the action is still refused. | S6-CLS-026 | `policy/gateway.py :: PolicyGateway` interface with `review(action) -> Optional[Classification]`; the engine consults it after local evaluation and its verdict is final. | `test_auto_classifier.py::test_external_policy_overrides_local_bypass` — stub the gateway to deny and assert the action is denied even with `bypassPermissions`. | A local bypass must not be able to overrule an organisational policy. Mitigation: the gateway verdict is applied last and is non-overridable by mode. | P2 safety-tier / Phase 3 |
| S6-SANDBOX-030 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §6, F-9, F-10; area8-permissions.md §6.1–§6.3 | A sandbox is an OS-level isolation layer that **substitutes for** the permission prompt rather than layering on top of it; two sandbox modes exist (auto-allow and a stricter one) and a documented set of things runs outside the sandbox. | S6-MODE-001 | `sandbox/runner.py :: SandboxRunner` with `mode: Literal['auto-allow','strict']`; `sandbox/policy.py :: outside_sandbox_rules()` enumerating the exceptions. | `test_sandbox.py::test_sandbox_substitutes_for_prompt` — assert no permission prompt is raised for a sandboxed action; `::test_exceptions_run_outside`. | Sandbox semantics differ per platform and are absent on native Windows (observed limitation L3). Mitigation: the sandbox capability is detected at runtime and its absence is reported, not silently ignored. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-SANDBOX-031 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 F-10, §6.4; area8-permissions.md §6.4, §6.5 | `sandbox.failIfUnavailable` is the control that stops a 'safe' sandbox silently degrading to an unsafe one: when the sandbox cannot be established and the setting is on, the action is refused rather than run unsandboxed. The unsandboxed retry is a **distinct, separately-gated** capability, not an ordinary tool call. | S6-SANDBOX-030 | `sandbox/runner.py :: ensure_sandbox() -> SandboxHandle` raising `SandboxUnavailable`; `sandbox/runner.py :: unsandboxed_retry()` requiring its own grant; `config/settings.py :: sandbox.failIfUnavailable`. | `test_sandbox.py::test_fail_if_unavailable_refuses` and `::test_unsandboxed_retry_is_separately_gated`. | A silent fallback from sandboxed to unsandboxed execution is the most dangerous kind of degradation. Mitigation: fail-closed by default with an explicit, separately-authorised escape. | P2 safety-tier / Phase 2 |
| S6-SANDBOX-032 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §6.6; area8-permissions.md §6.6 | Sandbox path syntax differs from permission path syntax; the two are separate specifications and the sandbox's own path forms are used for the sandbox's own decisions. | S6-RULE-014 | `sandbox/paths.py :: parse_sandbox_path(spec) -> list[PathPattern]` distinct from `permissions/pathmatch.py`. | `test_sandbox.py::test_sandbox_path_syntax_distinct` — assert a spec valid in one syntax is not accepted by the other. | Conflating the two syntaxes would either under- or over-restrict the sandbox. Mitigation: separate parsers with separate tests. | P1 extensibility / Phase 3 |
| S6-TRUST-033 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §8, F-14, F-15; area8-permissions.md §8.1–§8.3 | Workspace trust gates **grants** from a repository: a repository may contribute allow rules, but only once a human has trusted the folder. Trust is keyed by the resolved repository path and stored in the global configuration, not in a settings file, and the trust dialog lists explicitly what runs before you trust a folder. | S1-SESSION-018, S6-RULE-010 | `trust/workspace.py :: TrustStore` with `is_trusted(repo_path) -> bool` and `record_trust(repo_path)`; `trust/workspace.py :: TRUSTED_KEYS` enumerating what a grant unlocks. | `test_trust.py::test_untrusted_repo_grants_are_ignored` and `::test_trust_stored_in_global_config` and `::test_pre_trust_table_present`. | Trust is the boundary between a repository's instructions and the operator's machine. Mitigation: trust is stored outside the repository so the repository cannot grant itself trust, and the granted set is enumerated. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-BYPASS-034 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §7, F-16; area8-permissions.md §7.1–§7.4 | `bypassPermissions` is a one-way door: it requires a recorded acceptance (a dialog, or an explicit flag combination), is refused for a root user, and is refused by platform and policy guards. It does **not** bypass the critical-path circuit breaker, the protected-path check, or an organisational policy layer. | S6-CRIT-021, S6-PROT-020, S6-CLS-028 | `cli/flags.py :: require_bypass_acceptance(plan) -> None`; `permissions/modes.py :: bypass_guards()` returning the refusal reasons; `permissions/engine.py` applying the non-overridable checks after the mode decision. | `test_bypass.py::test_bypass_requires_acceptance` and `::test_bypass_refused_for_root` and `::test_bypass_does_not_override_critical_path`. | Bypass is the most dangerous mode; its limits must be encoded in the engine, not in a warning. Mitigation: the non-overridable checks run after the mode decision and are tested. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-RESTRICT-035 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 F-17; PROTOCOL-NOTES.md (tool-set mutation matrix) | `--restricted` is a coherent read-mostly mode: it removes the shell, PowerShell, fetch and monitor tools, empties the MCP server list, prunes the slash-command list, and **refuses** the dangerous permission modes. | S1-TOOLSET-020, S6-MODE-001 | `cli/flags.py :: apply_restricted(plan) -> None` removing the tools and raising a validation error if a dangerous mode was requested. | `test_restricted.py::test_restricted_tool_set` and `::test_restricted_refuses_dangerous_modes`. | A restricted mode that still allows a dangerous mode is not restricted. Mitigation: the mode combination is rejected at validation time. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-APPROVE-036 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §5, F-18, F-19; area5-bash-permissions.md F13 | The interactive permission prompt has a defined layout with options and key bindings, and the decision is reported in structured metadata. Some prompts deliberately withhold the 'don't ask again' option so that a saved rule cannot over-generalise. | S6-RULE-010, S1-INPUT-026 | `ui/confirm.py :: PermissionPrompt` with `options: list[Option]`, `render() -> Frame`, `handle_key(key) -> Decision`; `ui/confirm.py :: Option(label, action, persistable: bool)`. | `test_prompt.py::test_prompt_layout_and_bindings` and `::test_non_persistable_option_absent` — assert a prompt that must not persist has no 'always allow' affordance. | An over-general 'always allow' turns a one-off approval into a standing capability. Mitigation: persistability is a property of the option, and the test asserts its absence. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-APPROVE-037 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 F-7, §4 Area 5 F14; area5-bash-permissions.md F14 | A permission decision is reported in structured metadata attached to the tool result, and a denial carries the **full tool input** so the operator can see exactly what was attempted. | S6-DENIAL-010, S1-RESULT-011 | `protocol/toolresult.py :: ToolDecisionMeta(decision, reason, rule_id, tool_input)`; `protocol/envelope.py :: permission_denials: list[PermissionDenial]`. | `test_decision_metadata.py::test_denial_carries_full_tool_input` — assert the recorded denial includes the complete input object for a shell command. | Redacting the attempted input hides the risk from the operator. Mitigation: the full input is recorded; any redaction is a separate, explicit, audited projection (see S14). | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-DENIAL-038 | agent-reference-shots/tool-error-contract.json; agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 F-5 | A denial produces **two views of one event**: a `system`/`permission_denied` event carrying `tool`, `tool_use_id`, `decision_reason_type` and `decision_reason`, and a `user`/`tool_result` with `is_error: true` and the denial text. | S6-APPROVE-037, S1-CLI-005 | `protocol/events.py :: PermissionDeniedEvent(tool, tool_use_id, decision_reason_type, decision_reason, message)`; `runtime/loop.py` emitting both views from one `Decision` object. | `test_denial_event.py::test_both_views_emitted_from_one_decision` — assert the event and the tool result share the same `tool_use_id` and reason. | Two independently-generated views could disagree. Mitigation: both are projections of a single `Decision` object. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-DENIAL-039 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 6 F4, F9, §4 Area 8 §4.7; area8-permissions.md §4.7 | A denial's model-facing text is specific and actionable: it names the offending fragment, states what would have required approval, and states whether the action was performed. In the no-approval-surface case the text explicitly says the action was NOT performed, that the agent must not claim success and must not retry. | S5-EXEC-030, S6-APPROVE-040 | `permissions/engine.py :: denial_text(decision) -> str` with a structured template; `permissions/engine.py :: DenialText(reason, required_approval, action_performed: bool, remedy)`. | `test_denial_text.py::test_no_surface_denial_states_action_not_performed` and `::test_denial_names_offending_fragment`. | A denial that does not state whether the action happened invites the model to report a false success. Mitigation: the 'action performed' fact is a field of the denial, and the test asserts its presence. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-APPROVE-040 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 F-18, F-22; area8-permissions.md §5.1, §8.4 | Two independent 'no approval surface' mechanisms exist and behave differently: `--permission-prompts none` turns an unanswered prompt into a denial with an explicit 'the action was NOT performed' contract, while a headless run with no prompt surface treats the action as denied for the same reason but with a different framing. | S6-DENIAL-039, S1-CLI-008 | `permissions/engine.py :: no_surface_policy(kind: Literal['none','headless']) -> DenialText`; the two kinds produce different text but the same outcome (denied, not performed). | `test_no_surface.py::test_both_mechanisms_deny_not_perform` and `::test_texts_differ_but_outcome_matches`. | A prompt that silently disappears could be interpreted as an approval. Mitigation: both mechanisms deny explicitly and say the action was not performed. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-APPROVE-041 | area8-permissions.md §5.2, §5.3 | Approval persistence has a documented granularity: a session-scoped approval is offered for protected-path writes (project `.claude` folder / user `~/.claude` folder), and some prompts offer only a one-time approval. | S6-APPROVE-036, S6-PROT-020 | `ui/confirm.py :: Option(scope=Literal['once','session','project','user'])`; `permissions/engine.py` writing the chosen scope to the rule store. | `test_prompt.py::test_session_scoped_approval_written_with_session_scope` and `::test_one_time_only_prompt_persists_nothing`. | Persisting a broader approval than the user granted is a privilege escalation. Mitigation: the scope written is exactly the option's scope; a one-time option writes nothing. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-CANCEL-042 | area-interactive-mode/_section-interactive-mode.md §2 F-I8; agent-evidence.md §4 Area 1 F10 | A running turn can be interrupted: the work completed so far is kept, the tool call in flight is cancelled, and the turn ends with an explicit interrupted reason rather than an error. | S1-INPUT-031, S4-LOOP-011 | `runtime/interrupt.py :: InterruptController` with `request()`, `is_set`, and `on_cancel` hooks; `runtime/loop.py` checking the controller at each tool-call boundary and ending the turn with `TurnOutcome(status='interrupted')`. | `test_interrupt.py::test_completed_work_is_kept` and `::test_inflight_tool_is_cancelled` and `::test_terminal_reason_is_interrupted`. | An interrupt that discards completed work loses the operator's context. Mitigation: partial results are retained and the outcome is distinguishable from an error. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-CANCEL-043 | area-interactive-mode/_section-interactive-mode.md §2 F-I8, F-I2 | After an interrupt, queued messages are sent next; the interrupted turn's partial output remains visible and the queued entries are delivered in order. | S6-CANCEL-042, S1-QUEUE-030 | `ui/queue.py :: PromptQueue.deliver_after_interrupt()`; `runtime/loop.py` draining the queue into the next turn. | `test_interrupt.py::test_queued_messages_sent_after_interrupt` — queue two messages, interrupt, assert both are delivered in order in the next turn. | Losing a queued message after an interrupt is a silent data loss. Mitigation: the queue is durable in the session state and is drained on the next turn. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-RECOVER-044 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 6 F14; area6-tests-errors.md F14 | Recovery from a mid-response failure is an explicit, user-driven step: the operator replies `continue` (or, in `-p` mode, resumes the session and sends `continue`). The harness does not auto-resume. | S7-MIDRESP-010 | `runtime/loop.py` surfacing a `continue` affordance; `session/history.py :: resume_and_continue(session_id)` used by the headless recovery path. | `test_recovery.py::test_continue_resumes_the_turn` — assert a `continue` message after a mid-response failure produces a new turn that continues rather than restarting. | Auto-resume could re-run a tool that already executed (the duplicate-side-effect hazard). Mitigation: recovery is always user-initiated. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-PATH-045 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §3.4, §3.6; S2-ENV-020 | A tool call whose target is outside the working directories (and outside any added directory) requires approval, and the denial text names the working directories that were in scope. | S2-ENV-020, S6-DENIAL-039 | `permissions/engine.py :: path_in_scope(path, ctx) -> bool`; `permissions/engine.py` embedding the scope list in the denial text. | `test_path_scope.py::test_out_of_scope_path_denied_and_scope_listed`. | Path scope is the primary containment control. Mitigation: the scope list is computed from the frozen session context, not from the current process directory. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
| S6-HOOK-046 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 5 F15, §4 Area 6 F17, §4 Area 8 F-8; area5-bash-permissions.md F15 | A `PreToolUse` hook is the programmable extension point of the permission engine. It can return `allow`, `deny` or `ask`; a hook `deny` outranks an allow rule, while an `ask` from a hook and an `ask` rule both outrank `allow`. | S6-RULE-010, S6-APPROVE-036 | `hooks/dispatch.py :: PreToolUseDispatcher` with `run(event) -> HookDecision`; `hooks/schema.py :: HookDecision(decision, reason, updatedInput)`; the ordering rule: hook-deny > hook-ask > rule-ask > rule-allow. | `test_hook_ordering.py::test_hook_deny_beats_allow_rule` and `::test_hook_ask_beats_allow_rule` and `::test_rule_ask_and_hook_ask_both_beat_allow`. | A hook is operator-supplied policy; it must be able to *reduce* permission but a rule must not be able to *grant* past a hook's ask. Mitigation: the ordering is a single function with a table-driven test. | P0 parity-core / Phase 3 |
| S6-HOOK-047 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 6 F17; area6-tests-errors.md F17 | A hook's exit code is a compressed verdict channel: 0 proceeds, 2 blocks, any other code proceeds but reports. The blocking reason is the hook's stderr; the non-blocking notice carries only the **first line** of stderr. | S7-HOOK-018 | `hooks/exec.py :: run_hook(cmd, env) -> HookOutcome` with `HookOutcome(verdict=Literal['proceed','block','report'], reason)`; the verdict is a function of the exit code alone. | `test_hook_verdict.py::test_exit_codes_map_to_verdicts` — table-driven over 0, 2, 1 and 3; `::test_non_blocking_notice_is_first_line_only`. | A hook that crashes must not accidentally allow an action by default — hence the `onFailure: 'block'` opt-in for policy hooks. Mitigation: the verdict mapping is explicit and the fail-closed option is a separate setting. | P0 parity-core / Phase 3 |
| S6-HOOK-048 | area6-tests-errors.md F17 | A hook may set `onFailure: 'block'`, so a broken policy hook fails closed; a hook that times out reports 'timed out' rather than 'failed'. | S6-HOOK-047 | `hooks/schema.py :: HookSpec(onFailure: Literal['block','report'])`; `hooks/exec.py` distinguishing the timeout path from the failure path in the message. | `test_hook_verdict.py::test_on_failure_block_fails_closed` and `::test_timeout_message_distinct`. | The default (non-blocking) is what the reference does; failing closed is the safer default for policy hooks and is offered as an explicit opt-in. Mitigation: the option is a per-hook field, tested both ways. | P2 safety-tier / Phase 3 |
| S6-HOOK-049 | area6-tests-errors.md F17 (Windows-specific message) | On Windows a hook that requires Git Bash fails with a specific message when Git Bash is absent, and only exit codes 0 and 2 are then treated as verdicts; a hook can opt into PowerShell by setting `shell` to `powershell`. | S6-HOOK-047, S5-WIN-023 | `hooks/exec.py :: select_shell(spec) -> ShellFlavor` honouring `spec.shell == 'powershell'`; `hooks/exec.py` emitting the documented message when Git Bash is missing. | `test_hook_windows.py::test_missing_git_bash_message` and `::test_powershell_opt_in`. | A hook that cannot run must not be treated as having approved. Mitigation: the failure is reported with a specific message and only 0/2 count as verdicts in that mode. | P1 extensibility / Phase 3 |
| S6-SETTINGS-050 | agent-evidence.md §4 Area 8 §9.2, §9.3, F-20; area8-permissions.md §9.2–§9.4 | Permission rules live in settings files whose scope is explicit, and a small set of security-sensitive keys ignore the ordinary precedence stack so that a project cannot weaken them. | S2-SETTINGS-011, S6-RULE-010 | `config/loader.py :: effective_settings() -> Settings` with `RESTRICTION_ONLY_KEYS` handled before the merge; `config/loader.py :: SettingsScope` enum. | `test_settings_precedence.py::test_restriction_only_keys_ignore_project_scope` and `::test_scope_precedence_order`. | A project that could weaken a restriction would defeat the entire permission model. Mitigation: restriction-only keys are handled in a separate, earlier stage of the merge. | P0 parity-core / Phase 2 |
